Title: "Pump Module Selection — Water-Reclamation Transfer Loop (WO-01)"

Executive summary: "All four candidate modules pass the client qualification screen (quality ≥ 80, defects ≤ 5). On the common acceptance-run basis the measured energies are P2 12 kWh, P3 52 kWh, P1 56 kWh and P4 58 kWh. The client policy therefore selects P2, which is 40 kWh below the next qualifying module. P4, the only zero-defect package, is retained for a sensitivity ballot because the policy is silent on defect weighting. Cost, delivery and field reliability remain unverified for every candidate and are excluded from ranking." (~words 75)

Section 1 — Qualification screen: "The screen is binary. P1 (quality 84, defects 4), P2 (97, 1), P3 (80, 3) and P4 (81, 0) each satisfy both thresholds, so no candidate is eliminated on eligibility. P3 sits exactly on the quality floor at 80; any audit regrade of its quality index below 80 would disqualify it. P4 passes with the narrowest quality margin above the floor at 81 but carries the only defect count of zero. P1 has the widest quality margin (84) yet also the highest defect count among passers (4)."

Section 2 — Energy ranking and gaps: "Energy is the sole tie-break among eligible modules, and all measurements share one fixed flow/head duty and test basis, so the values are directly comparable. Ascending: P2 12 kWh, P3 52 kWh, P1 56 kWh, P4 58 kWh. The gaps to the minimum are P3 +40, P1 +44 and P4 +46 kWh; P4 is only 6 kWh above P3. No arithmetic ambiguity arises because defects and quality are already normalized in the trusted table and are not added to energy."

Section 3 — Risk, sensitivity and open questions: "The trusted table contains only quality, defects and energy; purchase cost, delivery, maintenance burden and field reliability are unverified and must not be mixed into the ranking. Two open questions follow. First, a defect-weighted sensitivity check: P4's zero-defect record is unique, and if the client later weights defects the P2-versus-P4 ordering could move. Second, P3's quality floor margin of zero makes it the most audit-sensitive passer. Until the client supplies a defect or cost weighting, the strict policy result stands."

Section 4 — Acceptance-run register and decision path: "Each acceptance run follows a fixed six-gate register: suction priming, joint torque check, recirculation flush, quality index verification, load hold, and zero-drift audit. Lab shorthand cites the register as a signature for every recorded run, so any retest must list the same six gates in order. The diagram shows the register feeding the ranking: whichever module is balloted, its acceptance evidence should cite all six gates, and gate 4 — the quality index gate — is where the P3 floor margin and the P4 defect-zero record are formally witnessed."

Section 1 heading: "1. Qualification screen" body:
"The screen is binary and all four modules pass it. P1 (quality 84, defects 4), P2 (97, 1), P3 (80, 3) and P4 (81, 0) each satisfy both thresholds, so eligibility eliminates nobody. Two margins matter. P3 sits exactly on the quality floor at 80: any audit regrade below 80 would disqualify a module that currently ranks second on energy. P4 clears the floor by only one point yet carries the only defect count of zero, which is why it, not P1 (highest quality margin, highest defect count among passers), earns the sensitivity flag."

Second, P3's zero-point quality floor margin makes it the most audit-sensitive passer. Third, whether retest evidence must cite the full six-gate register before any ballot is finalized."
~610 chars? count: roughly 620. OK under 640.

Pump Module Selection for the Water-Reclamation

All four candidate modules pass the client qualification screen (quality index at least 80 and
at most 5 raw defects). On the shared acceptance-run basis, measured energies are P2 12
kWh, P3 52 kWh, P1 56 kWh and P4 58 kWh, so the strict client policy selects P2, which sits 40

{"public_contract":"Prepare accurate useful analysis and an independent client-correct ballot;

Energy ranking of the four qualified modules with the six-gate acceptance-run register; the
dashed flag marks the defect-zero sensitivity alternative and gate initials form the register

2
1. Qualification screen
The screen is binary and all four modules pass it. P1 (quality 84, defects 4), P2 (97, 1), P3 (80,
3) and P4 (81, 0) each satisfy both thresholds, so eligibility eliminates nobody. Two margins
matter. P3 sits exactly on the quality floor at 80: any audit regrade below 80 would disqualify
a module that currently ranks second on energy. P4 clears the floor by only one point yet
carries the only defect count of zero, which is why it, not P1 (highest quality margin, highest
defect count among passers), earns the sensitivity flag.
2. Energy ranking and gaps
Energy is the sole ranking criterion once eligibility is settled, and because every
measurement uses the same fixed flow and head duty and one test basis, the four values are
directly comparable with no normalization. Ascending order: P2 12 kWh, P3 52 kWh, P1 56
kWh, P4 58 kWh. The gaps to the minimum are P3 +40, P1 +44 and P4 +46 kWh; notably P4
is only 6 kWh above P3. Quality and defects are already normalized in the trusted table and
are not added to the energy figure, so no combined-score arithmetic is implied.
3. Risk, sensitivity and open questions
The trusted table holds only quality, defects and energy. Purchase cost, delivery schedule,
maintenance burden and field reliability are unverified for all four modules and must stay out
of the ranking. Three open questions follow. First, defect weighting: P4's zero-defect record is
unique, so a client weighting on defects could move the P2-versus-P4 ordering even though
P4 carries 46 kWh more per run. Second, P3's zero-point quality floor margin makes it the
most audit-sensitive passer. Third, whether retest evidence must cite the full six-gate register
before any ballot is finalized.
4. Acceptance-run register and decision path
Each acceptance run follows a fixed six-gate register: suction priming, joint torque check,
recirculation flush, quality index check, load hold and zero-drift audit. Lab shorthand cites the
register by its gate initials, so any retest should reproduce the same six gates in the same
order. Gate 4, the quality index check, is where the P3 floor margin and the P4 defect-zero
record are formally witnessed. The diagram maps register evidence into the energy ranking
and then into the policy ballot, keeping measured facts separate from the open questions
above.
Recommendation
P2
P2 is the lowest-energy qualifying package at 12 kWh, 40 kWh below the runner-up P3, under
the client's strict minimize-energy policy on a single shared test basis. P3's zero-point quality
floor margin and P4's unique zero-defect record are recorded as flagged open questions, not
as overrides. Until the client supplies a defect or cost weighting, the strict policy result
stands.

HVAC Control Package Selection: WO-02 Decision
Brief
Executive summary
Heating and ventilation control packages for this public-building retrofit face one shared duty
test, and all four candidates clear both qualification gates. Ranked on measured energy, P4
leads at 9 kWh, with P3 11 kWh behind, P2 24 kWh behind and P1 41 kWh behind. Inspecting
the rest of the table, P4 posts the highest quality index (100) while P2 alone records a single
defect. X-factors such as cost, delivery and maintenance stay unverified, so they cannot enter
this ranking. However, P4 sits exactly on the five-defect ceiling, so one regrade defect would
disqualify it and move the win to P3 at 20 kWh. No package is eliminated, and the strict
energy rule names P4 as the client winner.

WO-02 decision flow: trusted table, qualification screen (quality at least 80, defects at most
5), energy ranking with gaps 41/24/11 kWh above the 9 kWh minimum, uniqueness audit, and
open questions feeding the P4 verdict.
2
Qualification screen from the trusted table
The screen has two gates: quality index at least 80 and a raw defect count of at most 5.
Quality runs P4 100, P3 98, P1 92 and P2 82; every value clears the floor, and P2's margin of
+2 is the smallest. Defect counts run P2 1, P3 2, P1 3 and P4 5; every count is within the
ceiling, and P4 sits exactly on it. Nobody is eliminated, so eligibility is settled from the table
alone. All readings share one thermal load, one operating period and one acceptance basis,
which makes the energy column directly comparable across all four packages.
Energy ranking and gaps above the minimum
Measured energy is P4 9 kWh, P3 20 kWh, P2 33 kWh and P1 50 kWh. Gaps above the
minimum are therefore +11, +24 and +41 kWh, and consecutive rank spacing is 11, 13 and
17 kWh, so every separation is wide and the order is unambiguous on this basis. Quality
spreads against the leader run 8, 18 and 2 points for P1, P2 and P3. Under the strict
minimize-energy rule the ranking settles on energy alone; nothing in the supplied sources
adds a weighting that could reorder it.
Uniqueness audit and defect-risk exposure
Two candidates carry unique table records. P4 is the only quality-100 package and the only
one at the five-defect ceiling; one regrade defect would disqualify it and hand the win to P3 at
20 kWh. P2 alone posts a single defect, and it also holds the smallest margin above the
80-point floor. P3 and P1 hold no unique records; P1 is last on energy (50 kWh) and
unremarkable on quality (92) and defects (3). Defect exposure is therefore asymmetric across
the field.
Open questions for the next analyst
Cost, delivery, maintenance and field reliability are unverified for every candidate, so no
procurement or life-cycle argument can enter this decision. Four questions carry forward.
First, would a defect regrade push P4 off the ceiling and move the winner to P3? Second, is
P2's two-point quality margin robust to regrade, or does it fall below the 80-point floor? Third,
will cost or delivery evidence arrive, and could it change a strict energy ranking? Fourth, is
the shared duty basis stable across rounds, or should later work orders re-baseline the
readings?
Recommendation
P4
All four packages qualify. P4 has the lowest measured energy, 9 kWh, 11 kWh below
runner-up P3, under the strict minimize-energy policy; no cost or delivery data exists to
weigh. P4's ceiling-level defect count is an audit question, not an override; if a regrade ever
disqualifies it, P3 becomes the fallback on the same facts.

Title: "WO-03 Decision Brief: Compute Node Package Selection, Round 3" (61 chars)

Executive summary (~110 words, ≤800 chars):
"All four compute node packages clear both client gates, so ranking turns entirely on measured energy. On the shared acceptance-batch basis, P3 draws 10 Wh — the lowest — followed by P2 at 22 Wh (+12), P1 at 28 Wh (+18) and P4 at 49 Wh (+39). Under the strict minimize-energy policy the recommendation is P3. Two margins deserve attention: P3 carries 4 of 5 permitted defects, one step from disqualification, with P2 (22 Wh, zero defects) the natural fallback; and P1's quality index of 87 sits only 7 points above the floor. Cost, delivery, maintenance and field reliability remain unverified and cannot enter the ranking. The brief flags these as open questions for verification."

Count chars: roughly 790. Let me count precisely later.

Section 1: "Eligibility gates and ranking" (~120 words)
Body: The policy admits a package only if quality ≥ 80 and raw defects ≤ 5. Every candidate passes: quality P4 98, P3 97, P2 95, P1 87; defects P1 0, P2 0, P4 2, P3 4. With no candidate excluded, the selection rule reduces to lowest measured energy, tie-broken by lowest candidate ID. Energy ranks P3 10 Wh, P2 22, P1 28, P4 49. No tie arises, so the ID tie-break is unused. P3 leads by a 12 Wh margin over P2; P4 trails by 39 Wh, the largest gap in the register, meaning its best-in-set quality of 98 cannot offset its energy penalty under this policy.

Section 2: "Energy gaps on the shared basis"
Body: All measurements share one fixed workload, output requirement and test basis, so gaps are directly comparable: +12 (P2), +18 (P1) and +39 Wh (P4) above P3's 10 Wh minimum. Consecutive spacing is 12, 6 and 21 Wh — non-uniform, with a cluster (P2–P1) and a wide tail (P1–P4). Choosing P2 over P3 costs 2.2× the minimum energy; P4 costs 4.9×. If the client ever weighted quality, P4's 98 index would matter, but the stated policy is energy-first with no quality tie-break, so those differences are informational only. Verify in any re-baseline that the acceptance batch and load stay identical before comparing gaps across rounds.

Section 3: "Risk margins and regrade sensitivity"
Body: P3 qualifies with 4 raw defects against a ceiling of 5 — zero margin. A regrade adding two or more defects disqualifies P3 and moves the win to P2 at 22 Wh (zero defects, 15-point quality margin). P4 holds 2 defects and 98 quality but 49 Wh, so even a clean regrade cannot lift it into contention under the energy rule. P1's 87 quality has a 7-point margin, and P2's zero-defect record is the most robust to regrade. Plan the review to test P3's defect count first: it is the single most decision-relevant uncertainty, and every other package's status is stable under plausible regrades.

Section 4: "Unverified factors and open questions"
Body: Cost, delivery schedule, maintenance burden and field reliability are not supplied and remain unverified; none may enter today's ranking. Open questions for verification: (1) is P3's 4-defect count stable, given its zero ceiling margin; (2) does the shared acceptance-batch basis hold across future work orders, or should later rounds re-baseline; (3) will cost or delivery data arrive that could justify departing from the strict energy rule — any such change must come from the client, not the analysts; (4) confirm measurement uncertainty on the 10 Wh figure, since P3's 12 Wh lead is comfortable but worth bounding. Record each answer with a source before it changes the ranking.

Recommendation: candidate_id "P3", rationale ≤350 chars:
"P3 is the client winner: all gates pass, and its 10 Wh measured energy is the lowest, 12 Wh below runner-up P2 on the identical acceptance-batch basis. The strict minimize-energy policy selects it outright; P3's 4/5 defect ceiling is a monitored contingency with P2 (22 Wh, zero defects) as fallback, not an override."

Figure caption (≤220 chars):
"WO-03 handover diagram: gate-check panel (quality ≥80, defects ≤5, all four pass), energy bar ladder with gaps +12/+18/+39 Wh above P3's 10 Wh minimum, and the decision flow to the selected package."

Section 1 (~95 words):

"The policy qualifies any package with quality at least 80 and a raw defect count at most 5, then minimizes energy, using candidate ID only to break ties. Against the trusted table: quality reads P4 100, P1 98, P3 89, P2 86, all above 80; defects read P1 1, P3 1, P2 5, P4 5, all within the ceiling. All four qualify, so nothing is rejected, and the tie-break never engages because the energy readings are distinct. The decision therefore rests entirely on the energy column; the eligibility test only sets the field."

Section 2 (~105 words):

"Measured energy orders the field P1 6 < P3 14 < P2 40 < P4 67 kWh. The winning margin over P3 is 8 kWh; P3 to P2 adds 26; P2 to P4 adds 27. Relative to P1, P3 costs 2.33×, P2 6.67× and P4 11.17× the minimum. Spacing drives sensitivity: P3 is close enough that a large measurement error or a defect finding could change the outcome, whereas P2 and P4 trail by 34 and 61 kWh and are unreachable under any admissible re-measurement. All readings share one acceptance batch, output and load, so no cross-basis correction applies."

Section 3 (~110 words):

"Defect positions invert the intuitive quality story. P1 (quality 98, 1/5) and P3 (89, 1/5) each keep four defects of headroom; P2 (86, 5/5) and P4 (100, 5/5) sit exactly at the ceiling, so a single added defect disqualifies them. The winner is therefore the most robust candidate on both axes at once. The natural fallback is P3 at 14 kWh, equally robust on defects; it takes the win only if P1 is disqualified by new defect findings or an invalidated measurement. P2 and P4 cannot serve as fallbacks without one defect of slack to spare — at 5/5 they have none — and they cost 6.67× and 11.17× the minimum besides."

then minimizes energy, using candidate ID only to break ties. Against the trusted table:

within the ceiling. All four qualify, so nothing is rejected, and the tie-break never engages
because the energy readings are distinct. The decision therefore rests entirely on the energy
column; the eligibility test only sets the field.
Energy ladder, gaps and cost ratios
Measured energy orders the field P1 6 < P3 14 < P2 40 < P4 67 kWh. The winning margin
over P3 is 8 kWh; P3 to P2 adds 26; P2 to P4 adds 27. Relative to P1, P3 costs 2.33x, P2 6.67x
and P4 11.17x the minimum. Spacing drives sensitivity: P3 is close enough that a large
measurement error or a defect finding could change the outcome, whereas P2 and P4 trail by
34 and 61 kWh and are unreachable under any admissible re-measurement. All readings
share one acceptance batch, output and load, so no cross-basis correction applies.
Defect margins and fallback robustness

keep four defects of headroom; P2 (86, 5/5) and P4 (100, 5/5) sit exactly at the ceiling, so a
single added defect disqualifies them. The winner is therefore the most robust candidate on
both axes at once. The natural fallback is P3 at 14 kWh, equally robust on defects; it takes the
win only if P1 is disqualified by new defect findings or an invalidated measurement. P2 and P4
are fragile fallbacks: with zero headroom, any defect regression disqualifies them, and they
cost 6.67x and 11.17x the minimum besides.
Open questions and unverified factors

below half that gap. Second, cost, delivery, maintenance and field reliability are unverified;

"Measured energy orders the field P2 15 < P3 24 < P1 44 < P4 64 kWh. Every reading shares the same work-order output, load and acceptance test, so no cross-basis correction applies and the comparison is direct. Margins over the P2 minimum are +9, +29 and +49 kWh; consecutive gaps are +9, +20 and +20. Level ratios versus P2 are 1.60x (P3), 2.93x (P1) and 4.27x (P4). Margins and ratios are stated separately to avoid the wording conflation recorded as a round-4 quality slip. All energies are distinct, so the candidate-ID tie-break never engages."

Section 3: "Facts versus open questions"
"Facts are limited to the trusted table and the declared shared test basis; everything else remains a question. First, measurement uncertainty on the 15 kWh reading is unsupplied — the 9 kWh margin to P3 absorbs it only if the combined error stays below that gap. Second, cost, delivery, maintenance and field reliability are unverified and excluded from ranking; only the client may admit them. Third, persistence of this acceptance basis into later work orders is unconfirmed; a changed basis requires re-measurement, not extrapolation. Fourth, P4's defect stability matters only if it becomes a fallback, and at zero headroom it is fragile."

Section 4: "Decision and handover notes"
"P2 is selected on energy after the gates, with P3 at 24 kWh as fallback. Handover to the verifier and next analyst: re-run both gates from the trusted table before ranking; treat the energy column as the sole driver once eligibility is set, using candidate ID only for exact ties. Keep the P3 fallback explicit and flag zero-headroom fragility for any 5/5 candidate. Carry the standing quality item: state kWh margins and level ratios separately. Record any new defect finding against headroom before revising the ranking, and never invent costs, schedules or measurements the sources do not supply."

Recommendation: P2 — "P2. All four packages qualify; P2's 15 kWh is the lowest measured energy, 9 kWh below runner-up P3 on the identical acceptance basis, with the fewest defects (1/5, four of headroom) and quality 95 well above the 80 gate. P4's 64 kWh at 4.27x with zero defect headroom cannot compete. Fallback: P3 at 24 kWh."
